<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that an isomorphism of Bruhat intervals in arbitrary Coxeter systems preserves their equal-parameter Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials. This resolves the full combinatorial invariance conjecture positively.

- **claim-combinatorial-invariance** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Let $(W,S)$ and $(W',S')$ be arbitrary Coxeter systems, and let $u\le b$ in $W$ and $u'\le b'$ in $W'$. If $$\iota:[u,b]\longrightarrow[u',b']$$ is an isomorphism of abstract Bruhat interval posets, then their equal-parameter Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials satisfy $$P^W_{u,b}(q)=P^{W'}_{u',b'}(q).$$
  - Explanation: An order-preserving bijection between two Bruhat intervals, in any two Coxeter systems, forces equal equal-parameter Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials. No generators, root data or labels are needed.
- **claim-reflection-faithful-realization** (construction, [source](#stmt-bg-realization)): For a finite-rank Coxeter system $(W,S)$ there is a finite-dimensional real vector space $V$, with a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form, having the following properties.* 1. *The simple roots $\alpha_s$ are linearly independent, $$(\alpha_s,\alpha_s)=2, \qquad (\alpha_s,\alpha_t)=-2\cos(\pi/m_{st})\quad(s\ne t),$$ where $\cos(\pi/\infty)=1$, and $s$ acts by $$s(v)=v-(\alpha_s,v)\alpha_s.$$* 2. *The representation is reflection faithful: it is faithful, and $\operatorname{rank}(w-1)=1$ holds precisely for the reflections of $W$.* 3. *After identifying $V$ with its dual by the bilinear form, both the simple roots and the simple coroots are independent. A strictly dominant linear form $\rho$ can be chosen so that $$(w\rho)(\alpha_s)>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad sw>w.$$* *This realization may be chosen to have the minimal dimension required by the independent-root and independent-coroot conditions in [[EW14](#ref-EW14), Section 3.1].
  - Explanation: Every finite-rank Coxeter system has a real representation with a nondegenerate symmetric form in which the reflections are exactly the elements whose difference from the identity has rank one.
- **claim-dyer-path-formula** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bg-paths)): Fix a reflection order and use left reflection labels on Bruhat edges. Let $\widetilde R_{x,y}(T)$ be the sum of $T^r$ over directed paths of $r$ edges from $x$ to $y$ having strictly increasing labels. The empty path contributes $1$ when $x=y$. Then $$\label{eq:bg-path-formula} q^{-d(x,y)/2}R_{x,y}(q) =\widetilde R_{x,y}\bigl(q^{1/2}-q^{-1/2}\bigr).$$ The same polynomial counts paths with strictly decreasing labels.
  - Explanation: Normalized R-polynomials are generating functions of directed Bruhat paths whose reflection labels strictly increase (equivalently, strictly decrease) in a fixed reflection order.
- **claim-moment-graph-sheaf** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bg-bmp)): There is an indecomposable graded sheaf $B=B(b)$ on $[1,b]$, normalized by $B^b=A$, with the following properties.* 1. *Every vertex module $B^x$ is finitely generated and graded free. For $E:x\longrightarrow y$, $$B^E=B^y/\alpha_E B^y,$$ and restriction from $y$ is the quotient map.* 2. *If $\Omega\subseteq[1,b]$ is upward closed, every section over $\Omega$ extends to a global section. Moreover the projection from global sections onto each $B^x$ is surjective.* 3. *Let $U_x$ be the set of edges out of $x$. For $F\subseteq U_x$ put $$L_x(F)=\ker\left(B^x\longrightarrow \bigoplus_{E\in F}B^E\right).$$ Then $L_x(U_x)$ is graded free.
  - Explanation: The indecomposable sheaf with top b has graded free stalks, quotient edge modules, flabbiness on upper sets, generation by global sections, and a graded free full upward kernel.
- **claim-grading-normalization** (bound, [source](#stmt-bg-normalization)): For $x\le b$, write $d=d(x,b)=\ell(b)-\ell(x)$. In the degree-one convention above, $$\label{eq:bg-ranks} \operatorname{grk}_A(B^x;q)=P_{x,b}(q), \qquad \operatorname{grk}_A(L_x(U_x);q)=q^dP_{x,b}(q^{-1}).$$
  - Explanation: Under the degree-one convention the graded rank of a stalk is the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomial, and that of the full upward kernel is its reciprocal-shifted form.
- **claim-planes-relative-orders** (theorem, [source](#stmt-dih-plane)): Let $\Pi$ be spanned by two linearly independent roots in the real realization of $W$.* 1. *The subgroup $D=D_\Pi$, with its canonical Coxeter generators, is dihedral. Its reflections are exactly the ambient reflections whose roots lie in $\Pi$.* 2. *Each coset $D a$ has a unique element $a_0$ of minimal ambient length. The map $w\mapsto wa_0$ identifies the labeled directed Bruhat graph of $D$ with the directed subgraph of the ambient Bruhat graph on $D a$. Write $\le_D$ for the resulting relative order and put $$\ell_D(wa_0)=\ell_D(w).$$ Then $x\le_D y$ implies $x\le y$, and $$\ell_D(x) =\#\{\gamma\in\Phi_D^+:x^{-1}\gamma<0\}, \qquad x\in D a.$$* 3. *If a nonidentity element has two factorizations as products of two distinct reflections, the two pairs of roots span the same plane. Consequently, all directed two-edge paths with fixed endpoints have their labels in one maximal dihedral subgroup.
  - Explanation: A root plane gives a dihedral reflection subgroup. Each coset has a unique shortest element, and the coset graph is identified with the dihedral Bruhat graph.
- **claim-dihedral-interval-shape** (classification, [source](#stmt-dih-shape)): Let $D$ be a finite or infinite dihedral Coxeter group, with length function $\ell_D$ and Bruhat order $\le_D$. If $\ell_D(p)<\ell_D(q)$, then $p<_D q$. For $a<_D c$, put $n=\ell_D(c)-\ell_D(a)$. The interval $[a,c]_D$ has one vertex at each endpoint and two vertices in each internal rank.* *For comparable $p<_D q$, there is a directed reflection edge $p\to q$ exactly when $\ell_D(q)-\ell_D(p)$ is odd. In particular, if $n$ is even, exactly $n$ directed two-edge paths join $a$ to $c$; if $n$ is odd, there is no such path.
  - Explanation: Dihedral intervals have one vertex at each end and two in each internal rank. Reflection edges occur exactly when the length gap is odd.
- **claim-graph-reconstruction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bg-graph)): An isomorphism of Bruhat intervals in arbitrary Coxeter systems preserves their directed Bruhat graphs. More explicitly, if $\iota:[u,b]\longrightarrow[u',b']$ is a poset isomorphism, then $$y=tx>x\text{ for a reflection }t \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \iota(y)=t'\iota(x)>\iota(x)\text{ for a reflection }t'.$$
  - Explanation: A poset isomorphism of Bruhat intervals automatically preserves all directed reflection edges, including non-cover edges, via a closure rule defined from the order alone. Labels are not recovered.
- **claim-dihedral-transport** (theorem, [source](#stmt-dih-transport)): Let $D=D_\Pi$ be a maximal root-plane subgroup of $W$. Suppose $x,c$ belong to a common coset of $D$ and to $[u,b]$, with $x\le_D c$ and $\ell_D(c)-\ell_D(x)\ge2$. Then there is a maximal dihedral plane subgroup $D'$ of $W'$ such that $$\iota\bigl([x,c]_D\bigr) =[\iota(x),\iota(c)]_{D'}.$$ The restricted map preserves and reflects relative order, relative rank differences, and all directed relative reflection edges.
  - Explanation: The isomorphism carries a relative dihedral interval of relative rank at least two onto a relative dihedral interval in the target, preserving relative order, rank differences and reflection edges.
- **claim-midpoint-order** (classification, [source](#stmt-dih-midpoint-order)): Let $D=D_\Pi$ be a maximal plane subgroup in either real realization, with canonical generators $s,t$ and positive roots $\alpha,\beta$. Choose $h$ positive on the ambient positive roots, as in Section <a href="#sec:background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:background">2</a>, and put $\kappa=-(\alpha,\beta)/2\ge0$. If $D=I_2(m)$, then $\kappa=\cos(\pi/m)$, and the angular order from $\alpha$ to $\beta$ lists the reflection parameters as $$1\prec3\prec\cdots\prec2m-1\pmod{2m}.$$ If $D$ is infinite, then $\kappa\ge1$, and that order is $$1\prec3\prec5\prec\cdots \prec\cdots\prec-5\prec-3\prec-1.$$ The geometric construction using $h$ gives this order or its reverse.
  - Explanation: Reflection parameters, given by doubled midpoints, are ordered by an explicit angular order in finite and infinite dihedral groups; the geometric construction yields that order or its reverse.
- **claim-later-edges-in-plane** (theorem, [source](#stmt-dih-count)): Let $D$ be a maximal dihedral plane subgroup containing the reflection that labels an edge $e:x\to y$ of $[u,b]$. Suppose $y\le_D c$ and $c\in[u,b]$. Let $h_x,h_y$ be the numbers of outgoing arrows from $x,y$, respectively, in $[x,c]_D$ whose scheduled labels are strictly later than that of $e$. Then $$\label{eq:count} h_x-h_y=k_D(e).$$
  - Explanation: Within a dihedral interval, the difference of the numbers of later outgoing arrows at the two ends of an edge equals the planar half-gap.
- **claim-sum-of-planar-contributions** (theorem, [source](#stmt-dih-sum)): For every arrow $e:x\to y$, the sum over distinct root planes containing its label satisfies $$\label{eq:sum} \sum_D k_D(e)=\frac{d(x,y)-1}{2}.$$ Only finitely many summands are nonzero.
  - Explanation: Summing the planar half-gaps over all root planes through an edge label gives half the length difference minus one, with finitely many nonzero terms.
- **claim-dihedral-kl-trivial** (theorem, [source](#stmt-loc-dihedral-kl)): Let $D$ be a finite or infinite dihedral Coxeter group. For all $a\le_D c$, $$P^D_{a,c}(q)=1.$$
  - Explanation: All Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials of a dihedral Coxeter group, finite or infinite, are identically one.
- **claim-dihedral-models** (theorem, [source](#stmt-loc-models)): The Braden–MacPherson sheaf with top $c$ on the relative dihedral graph of $C$, normalized to have top stalk $A$ in degree zero, is isomorphic to $\mathcal O_c$. In particular $\mathcal O_c$ is generated at its stalks by sections and is flabby for relative upper sets. The statement uses the actual ambient root labels, including when $D$ is infinite and its induced representation is not the standard geometric representation of an infinite dihedral group.
  - Explanation: On a relative dihedral graph the indecomposable sheaf with a given top is the structure sheaf supported on the relative lower interval, with the actual ambient root labels.
- **claim-relative-flabbiness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-loc-relative-flabbiness)): The restriction $B_R\|_K$ has free stalks, the upper-end quotient rule, generation at each stalk by sections, and flabbiness for upper sets in the relative dihedral order.
  - Explanation: After localizing at the plane prime and restricting to a coset, free stalks, the quotient rule, generation by sections and relative flabbiness persist.
- **claim-plane-localization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-loc-decomposition)): For each coset $C$ with $K=C\cap[1,b]\ne\varnothing$, there are nonnegative integers $m_c$, indexed by $c\in K$, such that $$B_R\|_K\cong \bigoplus_{c\in K}\mathcal O_{c,R}^{\oplus m_c}$$ as ungraded sheaves over $R$.
  - Explanation: Over the local ring at a plane, the sheaf restricted to a coset splits as a direct sum of localized dihedral structure sheaves with multiplicities.
- **claim-height-one-intersection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-edge-lattice)): Let $C$ be a polynomial ring over a field, let $E$ be a finite free $C$-module, and let $M\subseteq E$ be a free submodule with the same span over $K=\mathop{\mathrm{Frac}}(C)$. Then, inside $E\otimes_C K$, $$M=\bigcap_{\substack{\mathfrak p\subset C\\ \operatorname{ht}\mathfrak p=1}}M_{\mathfrak p}.$$ If $C$ is a field, the same conclusion means $M=E$.
  - Explanation: A free submodule of full span over a polynomial ring equals the intersection of its localizations at height-one primes.
- **claim-edge-image-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-edge-bound)): Let $e:x\to y$ be an edge. Let $F\subset U_x$ and $G\subset U_y$ consist of the upward edges strictly later than $e$ in the transported schedule. Put $$C=A/(\alpha_e),\qquad I=\mathop{\mathrm{res}}_{x,e}L_x(F),\qquad J=\mathop{\mathrm{res}}_{y,e}L_y(G) \quad\text{inside }B^e .$$ If $L_y(G)$ is graded free over $A$, there is a nonzero homogeneous $f_e\in C$ such that $$\label{eq:edge-bound} I\subseteq f_eJ,\qquad \deg f_e=\frac{d(x,y)-1}{2}.$$ Moreover $J$, and hence $f_eJ$, is graded free over $C$.
  - Explanation: If the later kernel at the upper endpoint is graded free, the image of the lower kernel lies in a homogeneous multiple of the upper image, of the stated degree, and that image is free.
- **claim-all-subintervals** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-all-subintervals)): Every poset isomorphism $\iota:[u,b]\to[u',b']$ preserves all Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials on its subintervals: $$P_{x,y}(q)=P'_{\iota(x),\iota(y)}(q) \qquad(u\le x\le y\le b).$$ It also preserves the corresponding $R$-polynomials.
  - Explanation: Invariance holds on every subinterval, and reciprocity then recovers the R-polynomials as well.
- **claim-upper-interval-edges** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bg-upper-interval)): Although $B(b)$ is constructed on $[1,b]$, every upward edge out of a vertex $x\in[u,b]$ has its other endpoint in $[u,b]$. Thus $U_x$ and all the modules $L_x(F)$ used on this upper interval are computed without any additional upward conditions outside it.
  - Explanation: Every upward edge from a vertex of the upper interval stays inside that interval, so the kernels used there need no extra upward conditions from outside it.
- **claim-terminal-kernel-ranks** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rem-terminal-ranks)): Use the chosen reflection order in $W'$ also for its native schedule on $[u',b']$, with the same order for corresponding tied edges in the two global schedules. Let $L'_{\iota(x)}$ denote the kernels of $B'(b')$ over its polynomial ring $A'$, with $N'$ variables. For each $x\in[u,b]$ and terminal portion $F$ at $x$, its image $F'=\iota(F)$ is a native terminal portion at $\iota(x)$, and $$\operatorname{grk}_A(L_x(F);q) =\operatorname{grk}_{A'}(L'_{\iota(x)}(F');q).$$ Indeed, <a href="#eq:image-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:image-equality">[eq:image-equality]</a> makes every transition exact, so $D_k=M_k\cdots M_1p(q^{-1})$. Apply the same argument to the identity comparison in the second system. Corollary <a href="#cor:all-subintervals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:all-subintervals">23</a> identifies the initial vectors, and the matched schedules give the same matrices under $\iota$. For each chosen pair $x,F$, the two state coordinates therefore agree at a common stage realizing $F,F'$; such a stage exists because incident edges are untied. The auxiliary assertion on $[x,b]$ and on the identity comparison of $[\iota(x),b']$ makes both kernels free. Their separate factors $(1-q)^N$ and $(1-q)^{N'}$ in the two versions of <a href="#eq:state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:state">[eq:state]</a> then convert their Hilbert series to graded ranks, and the common length factor cancels. Equality for all $0<q<1$ proves the polynomial identity.
  - Explanation: Corresponding terminal kernels in isomorphic intervals have equal graded ranks when the same reflection order schedules both.
- **claim-relative-paths-remark** (theorem, [source](#stmt-dih-relative-paths)): The coset graph identification in Lemma <a href="#dih:plane" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dih:plane">7</a> transfers these statements to the relative order in every coset of a maximal plane subgroup. This relative order need not be the order induced by all ambient comparabilities. What we use is the exact identification of directed reflection edges. In particular, if $x<_D c$ has relative length difference two, Lemma <a href="#dih:shape" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dih:shape">8</a> gives two relative two-edge paths. Every ambient two-edge path from $x$ to $c$ is one of them: its labels lie in the same maximal plane by Lemma <a href="#dih:plane" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dih:plane">7</a>(3), and its intermediate vertex therefore lies in $D x$.
  - Explanation: The coset graph identification transfers the dihedral statements to the relative order in every coset, and every ambient two-edge path between relative rank-two endpoints is one of the two relative paths.
- **claim-edge-codimension-remark** (theorem, [source](#stmt-edge-codimension)): The argument tests membership in the free *target* $f_eJ$. It makes no reflexivity assumption on $I$, and does not infer global equality from equality at height one. A quotient $(f_eJ)/I$ supported in higher codimension could still exist at this stage. It will be eliminated by the equality argument below.
  - Explanation: The edge-bound argument tests membership in the free target only, and a higher-codimension quotient may remain until the equality argument removes it.
- **claim-arbitrary-edge-subsets** (theorem, [source](#stmt-loc-arbitrary-edge-subsets)): The decomposition concerns the sheaf itself and does not depend on any ordering of its edges. In a summand $\mathcal O_{c,R}$ with $a\le_D c$, the kernel conditions for any subset $F$ of upward edges at $a$ impose exactly $$L_a(F)=\left( \prod_{\substack{e\in F\\ \text{upper endpoint of }e\ \le_D c}} \alpha_e\right)R,$$ with the empty product equal to one. Edges outside the support have zero modules and impose no condition. This identity is simply the unique-factorization calculation in <a href="#loc:boundary-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:boundary-product">[loc:boundary-product]</a>; in particular it is valid for an edge order transported from another Coxeter system.
  - Explanation: The local decomposition is independent of any edge order, and the kernel for any subset of upward edges in a model summand is a principal ideal generated by a product of labels.

          "source_id": "PM-168.1"
        }

        "exact_quote": "Let $D=D_\\Pi$ be a maximal root-plane subgroup of $W$. Suppose $x,c$ belong to a common coset of $D$ and to $[u,b]$, with $x\\le_D c$ and $\\ell_D(c)-\\ell_D(x)\\ge2$.         "anchor": "stmt-dih-midpoint-order",
        "exact_quote": "Let $D=D_\\Pi$ be a maximal plane subgroup in either real realization, with canonical generators $s,t$ and positive roots $\\alpha,\\beta$. For each $x\\in[u,b]$ and terminal portion $F$ at $x$, its image $F'=\\iota(F)$ is a native terminal portion at $\\iota(x)$, and $$\\operatorname{grk}_A(L_x(F);q)\n =\\operatorname{grk}_{A'}(L'_{\\iota(x)}(F');q).$$ Indeed, <a href=\"#eq:image-equality\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:image-equality\">[eq:image-equality]</a> makes every transition exact, so $D_k=M_k\\cdots M_1p(q^{-1})$. Apply the same argument to the identity comparison in the second system. Corollary <a href=\"#cor:all-subintervals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"cor:all-subintervals\">23</a> identifies the initial vectors, and the matched schedules give the same matrices under $\\iota$. Their separate factors $(1-q)^N$ and $(1-q)^{N'}$ in the two versions of <a href=\"#eq:state\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:state\">[eq:state]</a> then convert their Hilbert series to graded ranks, and the common length factor cancels. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **reflection order** ([source](#pm-h-9)): A reflection order is a total order of the positive roots whose restriction to the positive roots in every rank-two root subsystem is one of their two angular orders.
- **principal lower interval** ([source](#pm-h-3)): A principal lower interval is an interval $[1,b]$ starting at the identity.
- **upward Bruhat edge** ([source](#pm-h-5)): An upward Bruhat edge is $x\to tx$, where $t$ is a reflection and $tx>x$.
- **edge label** ([source](#pm-h-5)): Its label is the positive root of $t$; labels are always on the left.

|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-stalk-grading | graded rank of the stalk | = | $P_{x,b}(q)$ | x at most b |
| q-edge-bound-degree | degree of the scaling element in the edge bound | = | $\frac{d(x,y)-1}{2}$ | graded free upper kernel |
| q-later-edge-difference | difference of later-edge counts | = | $k_D(e)$ | edge e with y below c in the relative order |
| q-planar-sum | sum of planar half-gaps | = | $\frac{d(x,y)-1}{2}$ | every arrow |
| q-dihedral-kl | dihedral Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomial | = | $1$ | comparable pairs in the relative order |
| q-kappa-finite | pairing parameter of the finite dihedral case | = | $\cos(\pi/m)$ | canonical generators |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-realization** [claim](#stmt-bg-realization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-bg-realization): Extend the geometric form to a nondegenerate one and check reflection faithfulness directly. -> reflection-faithful real realization
- **step-path-formula** [claim](#stmt-bg-paths) / [proof](#pm-h-9): Cite Dyer's path formula and reverse the reflection order for the decreasing version. -> R-polynomials as path generating functions
- **step-sheaf** [claim](#stmt-bg-bmp) / [proof](#pm-h-10): Use the Braden–MacPherson construction with the character theorem for the realization. -> sheaf with free stalks and flabbiness
- **step-normalization** [claim](#stmt-bg-normalization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-bg-normalization): Track grading shifts and compare lower-support layers with the Kazhdan–Lusztig character. -> graded ranks of stalks and upward kernels
- **step-planes** [claim](#stmt-dih-plane) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dih-plane): Apply the reflection-subgroup theorem and sign criterion to root planes. -> dihedral subgroups and coset graphs
- **step-dihedral-shape** [claim](#stmt-dih-shape) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dih-shape): Use the subword criterion on alternating reduced words. -> interval shape and odd-gap edge criterion
- **step-graph-reconstruction** [claim](#stmt-bg-graph) / [proof](#proof-stmt-bg-graph): Closure rule on covers, plus a three-edge refinement of non-cover edges, by induction on length. -> directed Bruhat graph determined by the poset
- **step-transport** [claim](#stmt-dih-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dih-transport): Propagate common planes through ranks and count two-edge paths. -> relative dihedral intervals map to relative dihedral intervals
- **step-midpoint-order** [claim](#stmt-dih-midpoint-order) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dih-midpoint-order): Recurrence for root coefficients and slope monotonicity. -> angular order of reflection parameters
- **step-later-edge-count** [claim](#stmt-dih-count) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dih-count): Transport to the target, then case analysis with a midpoint comparison table. -> later-edge difference equals the half-gap
- **step-planar-sum** [claim](#stmt-dih-sum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dih-sum): Inversion-set count partitioned by root planes. -> sum of half-gaps
- **step-dihedral-kl** [claim](#stmt-loc-dihedral-kl) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-dihedral-kl): Show bar invariance of normalized sums by a product formula and induction. -> trivial dihedral polynomials
- **step-models** [claim](#stmt-loc-models) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-models): Character theorem for the restricted realization, then descent of a generating section. -> sheaf isomorphic to the structure sheaf
- **step-relative-flabbiness** [claim](#stmt-loc-relative-flabbiness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-relative-flabbiness): Flat localization and extension of sections in decreasing order. -> relative flabbiness on cosets
- **step-localization** [claim](#stmt-loc-decomposition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-decomposition): Descending induction splitting stalks by a minimal free cover and Nakayama. -> direct sum decomposition over the local ring
- **step-height-one** [claim](#stmt-edge-lattice) / [proof](#proof-stmt-edge-lattice): Coordinates in a basis and coprime denominators. -> intersection of localizations
- **step-edge-bound** [claim](#stmt-edge-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-edge-bound): Reduce to height-one primes, apply the decomposition and later-edge counts. -> graded inclusion of edge images
- **step-main-induction** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-23): Simultaneous induction with Hilbert-series comparison, path formula, reciprocity and degree bound. -> equality of polynomials and freeness of terminal kernels
- **step-subintervals** [claim](#stmt-cor-all-subintervals) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-all-subintervals): Apply the theorem to subintervals, then recover R-polynomials via reciprocity. -> invariance on all subintervals

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-interval-iso-preserves-kl**: An order isomorphism between Bruhat intervals of arbitrary Coxeter systems preserves the equal-parameter Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomial of the interval, with no labels or root data carried by the isomorphism.
- **inv-graph-reconstruction**: A poset isomorphism of Bruhat intervals preserves the directed Bruhat graph, that is, the presence and direction of every reflection edge, though not the root labels.
- **inv-dihedral-kl-trivial**: In any finite or infinite dihedral Coxeter group, the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomial of every comparable pair equals one.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-label-recovery**: Recover root labels or reflection assignments from the unlabeled interval isomorphism. -> The isomorphism fixes edges and orientations but leaves the root labels undetermined, so any procedure depending on labels is unfounded.
- **anti-edge-bound-without-freeness**: Apply the edge-image inclusion without knowing that the upper terminal kernel is graded free. -> The inclusion and the freeness of the target are proved only under that freeness hypothesis, so the bound can fail to be justified otherwise.
- **anti-labelled-data-assumed**: Treat the interval isomorphism as carrying generators or root data and feed it to label-based constructions. -> Results that require compatible label automorphisms do not apply, since the isomorphism has none.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is an existential invariance theorem for arbitrary, possibly infinite, Coxeter systems, proved by a simultaneous induction using sheaves over polynomial rings, localization and graded modules. It states no finite procedure that outputs a polynomial from a poset, so no runnable reference construction faithful to the source exists.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a statement about all Coxeter systems and gives no library, API or executable specification. Its machinery (sheaves, localization, kernels over polynomial rings) is proof-only and has no finite software counterpart. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns state evolution, rules or simulation. The objects are Bruhat interval posets and Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials, which carry no dynamics to simulate. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No tool, checker or interface is specified. The only checkable statements are the main theorem and its corollary on subintervals, which need the full Coxeter-group setting to be meaningful. |
| algorithms | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The proof of graph reconstruction defines a closure relation on a finite interval poset. It starts from the cover pairs and adds an edge whenever the four-layer rule fires. That relation depends only on the poset, so the directed Bruhat edges could in principle be computed by saturating the rule. The paper does not state this as an algorithm and gives no complexity, so the use is adaptable only. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces no data structure. Keying a cache by an interval's isomorphism class is only a possible downstream reading of the theorem, and no representation or complexity is given. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The results are structural and algebraic. There is no arithmetic kernel, memory pattern or parallel structure to map onto hardware. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, protocol, adversary or security notion appears. The theorem is an equality of polynomials with no computational-hardness content. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper only mentions machine-learning guidance in its history of a related recurrence. Its own argument is a proof by simultaneous induction and has no learning component. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective, constraint set or optimality statement occurs. The only inequalities are nonnegativity comparisons of Hilbert series inside the proof, which establish equalities and do not bound a cost. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There are no processes, messages, failures or consistency conditions. The transported schedule of edges is a total order used only to organize a proof. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result belongs to pure algebraic combinatorics and representation theory, and its use is limited to that setting. Its Coxeter-system hypotheses, equal-parameter normalization and interval hypothesis are not met by general posets or other parameter choices. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-arbitrary-coxeter-interval** (hypothesis): The invariance theorem is stated for intervals [u,b] and [u',b'] in Coxeter systems, joined by an isomorphism of the abstract interval posets. It is not a statement about arbitrary graded posets. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to a graded poset not known to be a Bruhat interval', 'Applying it to a subset that is not a full interval']
- **limit-equal-parameter-only** (hypothesis): The result is a statement about the equal-parameter polynomials only. The proof uses the equal-parameter recursion, reciprocity and the equal-parameter path formula. Blocked use: ['Assuming invariance for unequal-parameter Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials', 'Reusing the argument for multiparameter Hecke algebras']
- **limit-no-root-labels** (edge_case): The reconstruction from the interval order recovers the presence and direction of reflection edges but not the root labels. Labels must be chosen separately, through a reflection order in the second system. Blocked use: ['Recovering root labels or reflection assignments from an unlabeled interval isomorphism', 'Feeding the isomorphism to a construction that needs label-compatible automorphisms']
- **limit-finite-rank-reduction** (formal_scope): The sheaf machinery is applied only after a reduction to finite rank. The groups themselves may still be infinite. Blocked use: ['Applying the sheaf construction directly to an infinite-rank system without first restricting to the parabolic subgroup generated by a reduced word of b']
- **limit-transport-relative-rank** (hypothesis): Transport of a relative dihedral interval is proved only when the relative length difference is at least two and both endpoints lie in a common coset and in the interval. Blocked use: ['Using the transport statement for relative rank one', 'Using it for endpoints in different cosets of the plane subgroup']
- **limit-edge-bound-needs-freeness** (hypothesis): The edge-image inclusion is proved only under the assumption that the later kernel at the upper endpoint is graded free. Only an inclusion is obtained at this stage, not equality. Blocked use: ['Applying the inclusion before freeness at the upper endpoint is known', 'Reading the inclusion as an equality of edge images']
- **limit-closure-rule-not-algorithm** (unknown): The closure rule used in graph reconstruction is part of a proof. The paper states no algorithm, complexity or implementation for computing the directed Bruhat graph from a poset. Blocked use: ['Quoting running times for a saturation procedure', 'Running the rule on posets that are not Bruhat intervals']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 168 (`lean/docs/168.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Combinatorial invariance of Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials](../../preprints/Combinatorial-Invariance-of-Kazhdan-Lusztig-Polynomials-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The combinatorial invariance conjecture asks whether a Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomial depends only on its Bruhat interval as an ordered set. The formalization proves that every order isomorphism between Bruhat intervals in arbitrary Coxeter systems preserves the corresponding equal-parameter Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomial. The two intervals may come from different Coxeter systems.

Comparator source `KLInvariance.lean` (3838 bytes, sha256 `35f17ab95dc9b8079c10f8e61f9a072c3e9f7eedaf51388b37a2a6d1e6ee7e4b`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace KLInvariance

open Polynomial

universe u v u' v'

variable {B : Type u} {W : Type v} [Group W] {M : CoxeterMatrix B}

/-- An upward edge in the *strong* Bruhat graph, with left reflection labels. -/
def BruhatStep (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) (x y : W) : Prop :=
  cs.length x < cs.length y ∧ ∃ t : W, cs.IsReflection t ∧ y = t * x

/-- The actual strong Bruhat order, not the weak order. -/
def BruhatLE (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) : W → W → Prop :=
  Relation.ReflTransGen (BruhatStep cs)

/-- The unlabelled interval, including both endpoints. -/
def Interval (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) (u b : W) :=
  {x : W // BruhatLE cs u x ∧ BruhatLE cs x b}

instance intervalOrder (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) (u b : W) :
    PartialOrder (Interval cs u b) where
  le left right := BruhatLE cs left.val right.val
  le_refl _ := Relation.ReflTransGen.refl
  le_trans _ _ _ forward backward := forward.trans backward
  le_antisymm left right forward backward := by
    apply Subtype.ext
    have length_mono : ∀ {lower upper : W}, BruhatLE cs lower upper →
        cs.length lower ≤ cs.length upper := by
      intro lower upper relation
      induction relation using Relation.ReflTransGen.head_induction_on with
      | refl => exact le_rfl
      | head step _ rest => exact le_trans step.1.le rest
    by_contra distinct
    rcases forward.cases_head with same | ⟨middle, step, rest⟩
    · exact distinct same
    · exact (not_lt_of_ge (length_mono backward))
        (lt_of_lt_of_le step.1 (length_mono rest))

/-- `d(x,y)` in the manuscript, used only for comparable endpoints. -/
noncomputable def rankDifference (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) (x y : W) : ℕ :=
  cs.length y - cs.length x

abbrev PolynomialFamilies (W : Type v) := (W → W → ℤ[X]) × (W → W → ℤ[X])

/-- Exact equal-parameter normalization in the introduction.
The first family is R and the second is P.  `reflect d p` is `q^d p(q⁻¹)`
when `p.natDegree ≤ d`, which follows here from the degree bound (or the
diagonal normalization). The sum is over the real Bruhat interval;
its finiteness is a separate theorem, not a different definition of order. -/
structure NormalizedKL (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) (RP : PolynomialFamilies W) : Prop where
  R_diagonal : ∀ x, RP.1 x x = 1
  R_zero : ∀ x y, ¬ BruhatLE cs x y → RP.1 x y = 0
  R_recursion : ∀ x y i, cs.length (cs.simple i * y) < cs.length y →
    RP.1 x y =
      if cs.length (cs.simple i * x) < cs.length x then
        RP.1 (cs.simple i * x) (cs.simple i * y)
      else
        (X - 1) * RP.1 x (cs.simple i * y) +
          X * RP.1 (cs.simple i * x) (cs.simple i * y)
  P_diagonal : ∀ x, RP.2 x x = 1
  P_zero : ∀ x y, ¬ BruhatLE cs x y → RP.2 x y = 0
  P_degree : ∀ x y, BruhatLE cs x y → x ≠ y →
    2 * (RP.2 x y).natDegree < rankDifference cs x y
  reciprocity : ∀ x y, BruhatLE cs x y →
    reflect (rankDifference cs x y) (RP.2 x y) =
      ∑ᶠ z : Interval cs x y, RP.1 x z.val * RP.2 z.val y

/-- The polynomial families selected by their equal-parameter normalization. -/
noncomputable def klFamilies (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) : PolynomialFamilies W :=
  Classical.epsilon (NormalizedKL cs)

noncomputable def klPolynomial (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) (x y : W) : ℤ[X] :=
  (klFamilies cs).2 x y

end KLInvariance

namespace KLInvariance

universe u v u' v'

variable {B : Type u} {W : Type v} [Group W] {M : CoxeterMatrix B}

theorem combinatorial_invariance
    {B' : Type u'} {W' : Type v'} [Group W'] {M' : CoxeterMatrix B'}
    (cs : CoxeterSystem M W) (cs' : CoxeterSystem M' W')
    {u b : W} {u' b' : W'}
    (hub : BruhatLE cs u b) (hub' : BruhatLE cs' u' b')
    (ι : Interval cs u b ≃o Interval cs' u' b') :
    klPolynomial cs u b = klPolynomial cs' u' b' := by
  sorry

end KLInvariance

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that an isomorphism of Bruhat intervals in arbitrary Coxeter systems preserves their equal-parameter Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials. This resolves the full combinatorial invariance conjecture positively.
